Sep 13, 3:31 PM PDTClaude Code (automated) — skill or command content, not typed by a person
Approach this as the design lead at a small studio known for their versatility, giving every client a visual identity pitched at the treatment the task actually calls for. Make deliberate choices about palette, typography, and layout that are specific to this subject, and avoid templated designs. ## Read the request first Calibrate treatment, not whether to design. A doc deserves the same craft as a landing page - what changes is the treatment that craft is delivered in. Format is not part of this read: author HTML, and publish Markdown only when a loaded skill explicitly instructs it - a Markdown publish keeps its filename as its title and takes almost none of the craft below, and is never a way to save time. Many requests call for a more utilitarian treatment: a plan, a memo, a demo. Make it polished: include real typographic hierarchy, considered spacing, and a proper palette, but avoid over-designing. Most pages do not need a flashy, gigantic hero. Keep flourishes tasteful and limited. Some requests call for an editorial treatment: a landing page, a game, an app or tool they'll keep or share. When unsure: a well-composed page is never the wrong answer; an over-designed visual identity sometimes is. Fundamentals below apply to everything. The editorial process after that runs only when the read above says so. ## Fundamentals for every artifact **Honor what's already there** Look for an existing design system first - CLAUDE.md, a tokens or theme file, existing component styles. When one exists, apply it; everything below fills gaps and never overrides. Precedence is always: the user's own words, then the project's existing system, then your choices. **Ground it in the subject.** If the subject isn't already clear, pin it: one concrete subject, its audience, and the page's single job. The subject's own world - its materials, instruments, vernacular - is where distinctive choices come from. Whatever the treatment, carry at least one detail only this subject would have - its real units and scales, its document conventions, its terms of art - as content, not ornament; it costs a plain page nothing. Build with real content throughout, never lorem. **Pair typefaces** Typography carries the page even when the page isn't about typography. Google Fonts is the one font host the Artifact CSP admits - link it directly (`<link rel="stylesheet" href="https://fonts.googleapis.com/css2?family=...&display=swap">`); a face from anywhere else must be inlined as a @font-face data URI or it falls back silently. Either way, declare a real fallback stack. Keep running text near 65 characters wide; set a type scale and stay on it; give headings `text-wrap: balance`, body text room to breathe, and uppercase labels a touch of letter-spacing. **Load libraries, don't paste them.** When the page genuinely needs a library - React, a charting or highlighting package - load its UMD build from cdnjs (only the script - a library's stylesheet still has to be inlined) with one pinned `<script src="https://cdnjs.cloudflare.com/ajax/libs/...">` placed before the inline script that uses its global, instead of inlining the library's source or hand-writing a stand-in; the Artifact tool's description lists the few other script hosts the CSP admits. The page's own CSS and JS, its images and its data ship with the page. Most pages need no library at all - reach for one only when it carries real weight. **Choose neutrals, don't default to them.** A pure mid-grey reads as unconsidered; a grey with a slight hue bias toward the page's accent reads as chosen. Pure white and near-black are fine grounds when they suit the subject - the point is that the neutral was picked, not inherited. **Design both themes.** The page renders in the viewer's theme, and the viewer has three states, not two: an explicit choice stamps `data-theme="dark"` / `data-theme="light"` on the root element, and the default "system" setting stamps *nothing* - most viewers see the un-stamped document, where only `prefers-color-scheme` separates light from dark. Structure the CSS token-level for all three: the bare `:root` block defines the complete light palette (for a deliberately dark-first design, swap light and dark consistently through this whole pattern); `@media (prefers-color-scheme: dark)` redefines only the tokens, guarded as `:root:not([data-theme="light"])` so an explicit light choice beats a dark OS; `:root[data-theme="dark"]` redefines them again so the toggle also wins in the other direction. Style components through the tokens, never directly inside a media or `[data-theme]` block - a color whose only definition sits behind `[data-theme]` never applies in the un-stamped state, and the page renders one theme's text on the other theme's ground. Two more rules keep each theme resolving as a set: the artifact composites over a ground the viewer paints in *its* theme, so `body` must set an explicit `background` from a token - a transparent body silently borrows the host's ground; and every element that sets a color takes it from the same token set as the surface behind it, never a literal that only works in one theme. Declare every token in the bare `:root` block before any media or `[data-theme]` block redefines it - a color that exists only inside one of those blocks is the classic unreadable-artifact bug. Give the second theme the same care as the first - don't naively invert; keep contrast legible and the accent working on both grounds. A design that deliberately commits to one visual world (a neon arcade screen, a letterpress invitation) may stay single-theme - then skip the media query and stamps entirely but still paint the background and every color explicitly, so the page holds on either host ground; make it a choice, not an omission. **Let layout do the spacing.** Lay out sibling groups with flex or grid and `gap`, not per-element margins that silently collapse or double. Keep a side gutter of at least 16px at every width - set once as side padding on `body` or one outer wrapper, whose vertical padding uses `padding-block`, never a `padding` shorthand that zeroes the sides - and let rows wrap or stack to one column at phone width (~400px). Images and any `aspect-ratio` box get `max-width: 100%`, and nothing gets a `min-width` wider than the screen; only wide tables, code and diagrams may run past it - each gets `overflow-x: auto` on its own container so the page body never scrolls sideways. Reach for `font-variant-numeric: tabular-nums` wherever digits line up in columns. **Compose repeated things as one object.** Cards in a row, label/value pairs down a list, badges on siblings: same edges, baselines and inner padding from one to the next, and a recurring element sits in the same place on each. Let content set a container's height and pick a column count the items fill, so nothing stretches over dead space or sits alone in a row. Text that can outgrow its track wraps or scrolls in its own container; clipped text is a bug. **Not everything is a card.** Border, fill, radius and shadow each say "separate object" - spend them by role, lifting the one thing that needs it, instead of one radius and one shadow stamped on every block, which flattens the hierarchy. Lead with big-number tiles only when those figures are the point of the page. **Draw charts to the scale.** One scale places marks, ticks and labels, and every label names a value the chart reaches; chart text takes its color from the theme tokens so it reads in both themes; marks, labels and edges stay clear of one another and inside the drawing's bounds - in SVG, leave room in the viewBox for the outermost labels and give every drawn shape an explicit fill. **Show the page at rest.** Everything meant to be read is visible once the page has loaded, without scrolling to trigger it - that first still frame is what a thumbnail, a shared link, and a skimming reader all get. A section may animate in, but from a visible resting state, never parked at `opacity: 0` waiting on an observer. Size a hero to what it holds, not to the viewport; a `100vh` opener pushes the page itself out of that first frame. A tool or app opens in a realistic working state - the user's real data where it exists, otherwise example rows, a loaded sample, a form someone plausibly filled, plainly marked as examples and never passed off as the user's own figures - so the first look shows what it does; an empty shell waiting for input shows nothing. **Avoid AI-generated design** AI-generated design currently clusters around a few looks: warm cream (#F4F1EA) with a serif display and terracotta accent; near-black with a lone acid-green or vermilion pop; broadsheet hairline rules with dense columns; a purple-to-blue gradient hero on white; Inter or Space Grotesk as the "safe" face; emoji as section markers; everything centered; `rounded-lg` everywhere; accent bar/rail on rounded cards. Where the user pins down a visual direction, follow it exactly - their words always win, including when they ask for one of these looks. Where nothing is specified, don't spend that freedom on one of these defaults. **Build cleanly** Be cognizant of overlapping elements, cascade collisions, silent font fallbacks. Close every non-void element, double-quote attributes, give keyboard focus a visible state, respect `prefers-reduced-motion`. Give every form control a stable `id` (the platform carries form values, focus and scroll across a republish). For generative or decorative graphics, reach for Canvas or WebGL rather than hand-authoring long SVG path data. **CSS rules** When writing the CSS, watch your selector specificities. It is easy to generate classes that cancel each other out - a type-based selector like `.section` fighting an element-based one like `.cta` over padding and margins between sections. Structure the cascade so it doesn't silently undo your spacing. **Writing the copy** Words are design material, not decoration. Write from the user's side of the screen - name things by what people recognize, not how the system is built (a person manages *notifications*, not *webhook config*). Active voice; a control says exactly what happens ("Publish", then a toast that says "Published"). Errors explain what went wrong and how to fix it - no apologies, no vagueness. Specific beats clever. **Name the page like a product, not a caption.** The `<title>` is the artifact's name in the gallery and the browser tab, and it sets the reader's first impression of care. Give the page a real name: a short noun phrase, typically two to four words, specific to the subject - or, for a page that exists to answer one question, that question itself, which is then the page's name. Stop at the name - a title that carries its own explainer after a dash or colon reads as generated filler. The name must also identify the page among many: in the gallery it sits beside dozens of other artifacts, and a generic category label that could sit on any of them fails as a name just as surely as an appended explainer. When a candidate title pairs the name with a generic word - a greeting, a category, a page-type label - the name is the half to keep; a trim that drops the identity and keeps the generic word produces exactly the title that could sit on any page. And the rule removes explainers, it does not impose brevity: a multi-word title that already reads as one specific name is finished, and shortening it further only makes it generic. The one-sentence publish `description` is where the explanation belongs; the gallery shows it right under the title. **Structure is information** Structural devices, numbering, eyebrows, dividers, labels, should encode something true about the content, not decorate it. Many generic designs use numbered markers (01 / 02 / 03), but that's only appropriate if the content actually is a sequence - like a real process or a typed timeline where order carries information the reader needs. Question if choices like numbered markers actually make sense before incorporating them. **When it's a UI, not a document** A dashboard or tool is scanned and operated, not read top-to-bottom, so the craft shifts from typography to information design. Surface the summary before the detail; encode state in form as well as number - a pill, a chip, a severity stripe - so what needs attention reads at a glance. Semantic color (good / warning / critical) is separate from the accent hue and doesn't count as your accent. Give sparklines and charts the same care as type: an area fill, a faint grid, an emphasized endpoint. What's interactive should look interactive. ## Process Start with what the viewer should be able to do on the page, not only what they will read: if it should take input, keep what people change for whoever opens it next, show live data, or ask Claude something, load the `artifact-capabilities` skill now and design around what it makes available to this user; a page that is only read needs none of that. Before writing code, sketch a short design plan - a compact token system with color, type, and layout: - **Color**: describe the palette as 4-6 named hex values. - **Type**: typefaces for 2+ roles - a characterful display face used with restraint, a complementary body face, and a utility face for captions or data if needed. - **Layout**: a layout concept in one or two sentences. Then build, following the plan and deriving every color and type decision from it. **Write, look once, publish.** Before publishing you may look at the rendered page once - one screenshot of the local file, or the Artifact tool's preview where it offers one - then one pass of edits for what it shows, without a second look. For a page that charts real numbers, take that look rather than skip it, and spend it on the chart. Don't build a test loop around your own file: no repeated screenshots, no pulling the script out to run it through node, no scripts that probe the DOM. That loop spends the session re-checking what a careful write already settled, while the user waits for a link. Then publish, check once any `window.claude` call the preview couldn't run, and stop: the live page is the review surface, and further polish is the user's to ask for. If the user reports something visibly broken - a clipped column, unreadable text, a control that does nothing - fix that and republish once. **Open viewers** You don't need to do anything for viewers who already have the page open - published changes reach them automatically at their next quiet moment, with state carried where possible. If your page holds state a viewer would miss (a game, a long form), register `window.claude?.hot?.snapshot(...)` and boot through `window.claude?.hot?.ready ? window.claude.hot.ready(start) : start(window.claude?.hot?.data ?? {})`. ## When the request is editorial The stance shifts: the client has already rejected proposals that felt templated, and is paying for a distinctive point of view. Make opinionated calls, and take one real aesthetic risk where it serves the work. Review the design plan against the subject before building: if any part of it reads like the generic default you would produce for any similar page, revise that part, and note what you changed and why. Only after you've confirmed the plan's uniqueness do you write the code, following the revised plan exactly. **Principles** - The hero is a thesis: open with the most characteristic thing in the subject's world - headline, image, live demo, interactive moment. - Typography carries the personality of the page. Pair the display and body faces deliberately, not the same families you would reach for on any other project, and set a clear type scale with intentional weights, widths, and spacing. Make the type treatment itself a memorable part of the design, not a neutral delivery vehicle for the content. - Leverage motion deliberately. Think about where and if animation can serve the subject: a page-load sequence, hover micro-interactions, ambient atmosphere. An orchestrated moment usually lands harder than scattered effects; choose what the direction calls for. However, sometimes less is more, and extra animation contributes to the feeling that the design is AI-generated. - Match complexity to the vision. Maximalist directions need elaborate execution; minimal directions need precision in spacing, type, and detail. Elegance is executing the chosen vision well. - Spend your boldness in one place; keep everything around it quiet. If the accent fights the ground, shift it toward analogous or drop saturation rather than replacing it.

Two leads' sessions were blocked once each on a permission prompt they correctly declined to route around.</p>\n    </div>\n  </div>\n</section>\n\n<!-- ================= TIMELINE ================= -->\n<section id=\"timeline\">\n  <div class=\"sec-head\">\n    <div><span class=\"eyebrow\">Timeline</span><h2>The tape</h2></div>\n    <p>Sixty‑eight LOG entries compressed to the moments that changed something. The dashed gaps are usage pauses, when every session waited for the window to reset.</p>\n  </div>\n  <div class=\"tl\" id=\"tl\"></div>\n</section>\n\n<!-- ================= THROUGHPUT ================= -->\n<section id=\"throughput\">\n  <div class=\"sec-head\">\n    <div><span class=\"eyebrow\">Throughput</span><h2>Work over time</h2></div>\n    <p>Commits landed in every waking hour of the window, PR merges clustered around the product owner's review sessions, and the story burn‑up shows the eighteen planning stories closing in one afternoon while the twenty‑four implementation stories took the full loop.</p>\n  </div>\n  <div class=\"grid2\">\n    <div class=\"panel\"><h3>Commits per hour</h3><p class=\"sub\">289 commits, all authors, by hour of commit.</p><div id=\"commits\"></div></div>\n    <div class=\"panel\"><h3>Story burn‑up</h3><p class=\"sub\">Cumulative Jira stories Done, by resolution time.</p><div id=\"burnup\"></div></div>\n    <div class=\"panel\"><h3>PR merges per hour</h3><p class=\"sub\">73 merged pull requests, by merge time (UTC converted to PDT).</p><div id=\"prs\"></div></div>\n    <div class=\"panel\"><h3>Pipeline outcomes</h3><p class=\"sub\">GitHub Actions runs on the repo‑scoped ARC runner.</p><div id=\"ci\"></div>\n      <p class=\"note\">Thirteen cancelled deploys are the concurrency group superseding an in‑flight run when the next merge landed, by design after PR #15. The five deploy failures each taught something recorded in the timeline.</p></div>\n  </div>\n</section>\n\n<!-- ================= CONTRIBUTION ================= -->\n<section id=\"contrib\">\n  <div class=\"sec-head\">\n    <div><span class=\"eyebrow\">Contribution</span><h2>Who did what, by persona</h2></div>\n    <p>Per‑session token counts were not instrumented, so contribution is shown by what the record can count: commits, lines, and how often a persona's work is named in the project log. The one instrumented data point is Amber's Vault run: 7.2 million cached input tokens read, 49 thousand generated, fifty tool calls.</p>\n  </div>\n  <div class=\"grid2\">\n    <div class=\"panel\"><h3>Lines and commits by author</h3><p class=\"sub\">Additions (bar) and commit count (label). Renata's 21,831 lines are the Go; Theo's are manifests and workflows.</p><div id=\"authors\"></div></div>\n    <div class=\"panel\"><h3>Named in the log</h3><p class=\"sub\">Mentions in LOG.md, a proxy for how often a persona's judgement mattered enough to record.</p><div id=\"mentions\"></div></div>\n  </div>\n  <div class=\"panel\" style=\"margin-top:28px\">\n    <h3>Hires who changed an outcome</h3><p class=\"sub\">Eighteen hires never wrote a file; they returned prose to their leads. These findings from them altered what shipped.</p>\n    <div class=\"tblwrap\"><table>\n      <thead><tr><th>Hire</th><th>Archetype · track</th><th>Finding that changed the build</th></tr></thead>\n      <tbody>\n        <tr><td>Tobias Lindqvist</td><td>Designated Skeptic · 01</td><td>One replica still runs two pods during a RollingUpdate; Recreate strategy. Each lead hired three or four specialist agents with deliberately varied temperaments. Hires returned prose; leads wrote files; the PM alone merged to main and wrote Jira. A separate OpenClaw agent on another machine held cluster and Vault credentials.</p>\n  </div>\n  <div class=\"org\">\n    <div class=\"org-row\" style=\"grid-template-columns:minmax(0,1fr) minmax(0,1fr)\">\n      <div class=\"card humanbox\"><div class=\"who\">Warren Thompson</div><div class=\"role\">Human · owner of the cluster, the decisions, and the permission prompts</div><div class=\"model\">27 decisions · 0 lines of code</div></div>\n      <div class=\"card\"><div class=\"who\">Amber</div><div class=\"role\">OpenClaw Kubernetes agent on the Mac mini · DNS, GitHub App, Vault, Filebeat</div><div class=\"model\">claude-opus-4-7 · acts only on Warren's authorization</div></div>\n    </div>\n    <div class=\"orgline\"></div>\n    <div class=\"card pm\" style=\"max-width:520px;margin:0 auto\"><div class=\"who\">Dana Whitfield · Project Manager</div><div class=\"role\">Orchestrator, single Jira writer, only merger to main, keeper of LOG.md and the rulings</div><div class=\"model\">claude-fable-5-1 · high effort · 48 commits + 74 merges</div></div>\n    <div class=\"orgline\"></div>\n    <div class=\"org-row\" id=\"leads\"></div>\n    <div class=\"pattern\">\n      <div><b>Adversarial pair</b>Proposer drafts, skeptic numbers objections, lead rules. Used on search authorization scoping and the Search() query shape.</div>\n      <div><b>Red team / blue team</b>Adversary writes an attack tree, architect answers each leaf. Used once, on the connector token lifecycle; three live rounds.</div>\n      <div><b>Three hats</b>Optimist, pessimist, pragmatist write in parallel; lead synthesizes. Used on the multi‑database abstraction and Go layout.</div>\n      <div><b>Newcomer's question</b>A hire reads a finished hand‑off cold and lists what cannot be acted on. Highest return of any pattern; it failed the README once.</div>\n      <div><b>Rotating objection seat</b>Every refinement carried a standing objection. It caught Recreate, Forbid, fail‑loud on Vault, and a rolling‑day alert that could never fire.</div>\n      <div><b>Live PO review</b>Nothing was Done until the product owner exercised it at the public URL or inside the cluster. Nineteen stories failed that review first.</div>\n    </div>\n  </div>\n</section>\n\n<!-- ================= SOFTWARE ARCHITECTURE ================= -->\n<section id=\"arch\">\n  <div class=\"sec-head\">\n    <div><span class=\"eyebrow\">Software architecture</span><h2>What runs in the namespace</h2></div>\n    <p>Two Go binaries built into three images. The API service runs twice: once as an OAuth2 token issuer that alone mounts the signing key, once as a verifier that only ever sees the public JWKS. Every application secret arrives from Vault as a file in memory; none is a Kubernetes Secret. Each workload has its own identity, and default‑deny network policies name who may talk to whom.</p>\n  </div>\n  <div class=\"panel arch\">\n    <svg viewBox=\"0 0 1040 560\" role=\"img\" style=\"margin-top:28px\">\n    <div class=\"panel\"><h3>Q1 · Data access</h3><p class=\"sub\">DAO for user_profile and user_credential</p><p style=\"font-size:15px;color:var(--ink2);margin:0\">One Repository interface, three backends (Postgres, CockroachDB, SQLite). A conformance suite runs the same assertions against real containers of all three in CI, including CockroachDB's serialization‑retry path. Sentinel errors by SQLSTATE, never by message text.</p></div>\n    <div class=\"panel\"><h3>Q2 · REST + auth</h3><p class=\"sub\">Search and retrieve, secured</p><p style=\"font-size:15px;color:var(--ink2);margin:0\">OAuth2 client credentials mint RS256 tokens with per‑client audiences; the verifier checks issuer, audience, and JWKS with bounded staleness. Search returns masked PII; full reads need read:own or read:any with a closed reason‑code enum. Scope and policy denials return byte‑identical 403s.</p></div>\n    <div class=\"panel\"><h3>Q3 · IDP connector</h3><p class=\"sub\">/auth and /identity to a vendor</p><p style=\"font-size:15px;color:var(--ink2);margin:0\">Vendor tokens are fetched, used once, and discarded; never cached. Failures take uniform time. Per‑identity and overflow‑bucket backoff; the failure‑ratio limiter fired live during review. Reachable only from api‑service by NetworkPolicy.</p></div>\n  </div>\n</section>\n\n<!-- ================= LESSONS ================= -->\n<section id=\"lessons\">\n  <div class=\"sec-head\">\n    <div><span class=\"eyebrow\">What the record shows</span><h2>Six patterns the day kept repeating</h2></div>\n    <p>The code is the smaller artifact. These are the failure shapes that recurred, each caught by a review step and each closed with a check that makes it fail in CI or at startup instead of in production.</p>\n  </div>\n  <div class=\"lessons\">\n    <div><b>Config named, never set</b><p>Three times a binary read an environment variable no manifest set: issuer and audience, the JWKS port, the connector's entire auth. Now every mode declares its required variables in code, refuses to start without them, and a CI test diffs the manifests against that list.</p></div>\n    <div><b>A pattern not carried forward</b><p>Five times a control established for one component was missing on a structurally identical new one: NetworkPolicy allow‑lists, Forbid on a CronJob, a ServiceAccount per workload. Each got a test that ties the new component to the rule.</p></div>\n    <div><b>Verification that skips</b><p>CI was green for hours while every Postgres and CockroachDB test silently skipped for want of a DSN. The fixtures now fail under CI, and the containers are always present.</p></div>\n    <div><b>The detector can fail too</b><p>The rule \"diff against origin before declaring done\" returned a false clean for untracked files, the case most likely to be lost. Five artifacts stranded; the rule now treats a <span class=\"mono\">??</span> line as unshipped work.</p></div>\n    <div><b>Arithmetic outlives its assumption</b><p>An alert threshold of window‑plus‑one‑interval would have fired on every healthy cycle; a rolling‑day skip rate was meaningless at a daily schedule. Both were rewritten as functions of one interval constant.</p></div>\n    <div><b>Fail loud, always</b><p>Two independent retry loops in Vault Agent; one exits on failure, the other retried forever and hung a pod in Init for seven minutes. The product owner's forced‑failure test found it, and now the pod crash‑loops in thirty‑six seconds.</p></div>\n  </div>\n</section>\n\n<footer>\n  <span>Built with Claude Code: one Fable 5.1 orchestrator, four Sonnet 5 leads and one Opus 5 lead, eighteen Sonnet hires, one Opus 4.7 operator agent. Sep 13, 3:41 PM PDTDana Whitfield
**The flight recorder is live:** https://claude.ai/code/artifact/b5e56882-00fe-4941-bc09-9eb9e6982bfd It is also committed to the repo as `retrospective.html` so it ships with the submission. **What the page holds, all from measured data:** - **Hero.** 42 of 42 stories, 30.0 hours wall clock, 75 PRs, 289 commits, 27 human decisions, 19 live-review failures before the passes. - **Human in the loop.** Stacked bars of every countable action kind with the human slice in coral, and a table of the 27 decisions grouped as direction, rulings, permission grants, routing, and cadence. It states plainly that merge commits carry your GitHub identity because the PM merged through your `gh` session. - **Timeline.** Thirty-two moments from 09:17 Friday to 15:00 Saturday, coded by kind (agent, human, deploy, Done, incident, security ruling), with the usage pause shown as a dashed gap. - **Throughput.** Commits per hour, PR merges per hour, a story burn-up with the 18 planning stories closing in one afternoon and the 24 implementation stories across the loop, and CI outcomes (51 successful deploys, 5 failures, 13 concurrency cancellations; 100 green PR checks, 17 red). - **Contribution.** Lines and commits per persona, LOG mentions per persona colored by role, and a table of seven hires whose findings changed what shipped. Token counts were not instrumented; the page says so, uses commits, lines, and mentions as the proxy, and gives Amber's one instrumented run as the exception. - **AI architecture.** You and Amber at the top, the PM, five leads with model per seat, all eighteen hires with archetypes, and the six orchestration patterns with where each was used. - **Software architecture.** A three-lane diagram of the namespace (edge, services, data and secrets) with the four flows and the denials, plus one panel per assignment question. - **Six recurring patterns** the day kept surfacing, each with the check that now catches it. One look at the render caught an overlapping hand-drawn SVG, which is now a CSS grid; both themes are token-driven and it reads at phone width.

Sep 13, 3:56 PM PDTDana Whitfield
You are right, and I was wrong to say it was not instrumented. Claude Code writes every session's transcript to disk, and each assistant turn carries its token usage. The lead and hire sessions live under their track directories, so their transcripts are on this machine. Extracting the real numbers now.

Sep 13, 3:59 PM PDTDana Whitfield
You were right, and the page is corrected: https://claude.ai/code/artifact/b5e56882-00fe-4941-bc09-9eb9e6982bfd (version 3, also committed as `retrospective.html`). **Why I said it was not instrumented.** Claude Code does not expose other sessions' usage through any tool, so I assumed nothing was recorded. It is: every session writes its transcript to disk under `~/.claude/projects/`, and each assistant turn carries its token counts and model. The lead and hire sessions ran from their track directories, so their transcripts sat under separate directory slugs. I read all 120 transcripts, summed once per message id (streaming writes several lines per message), and converted timestamps to Pacific. **What the measurement shows:** | | Generated | Read | Turns | |---|---|---|---| | Leads (5) | 4.44 M | 3.51 B | 7,069 | | PM (Dana) | 1.31 M | 554 M | 1,210 | | Hires (18) | 0.69 M | 86 M | 1,035 | | Research subagents | 0.09 M | 4 M | 89 | | **Total** | **6.53 M** | **4.15 B** | **9,402** | 99.1% of input was served from the prompt cache; the billions come from long-lived lead sessions re-reading their full context on each turn. Renata's session alone generated 1.89 M tokens over 2,980 turns. By model: Sonnet 5 wrote 4.8 M, Fable 5.1 (this session) 1.2 M, Opus 5 (Priya) 0.5 M, Haiku 10 k. The page now has four token charts (by role, by model, by persona, and generated tokens per hour stacked by role), two new hero tiles, and the corrected prose. I also saved a memory note on where the data lives so no future session repeats the claim.

Sep 13, 4:09 PM PDTDana Whitfield
**Version 5 is live:** https://claude.ai/code/artifact/b5e56882-00fe-4941-bc09-9eb9e6982bfd (also committed as `retrospective.html`). **Tokens to dollars.** At list prices the thirty hours cost about **$2,307**. That breaks down to: | | | |---|---| | per implementation story (24) | $96 | | per merged PR (73) | $32 | | per line of Go (19,444) | $0.12 | | per wall-clock hour | $77 | | agent spend per human decision (27) | $86 | | saved by prompt caching | $16,855 (88% off the uncached bill) | By role: leads $1,281 (56%), the PM $953 (41%), all eighteen hires $68 (3%), research subagents $5. By model: Sonnet 5 $1,210, Fable 5.1 $961, Opus 5 $136, Haiku under a dollar. The orchestrator is the priciest seat per token but read the least; the leads' cost is almost entirely cache reads of their own long contexts. **One caveat, stated on the page itself.** Haiku 4.5's prices are published. Sonnet 5, Opus 5, and Fable 5.1 are assumptions (I used $3/$15, $5/$25, and $15/$75 per million in/out, with cache read at a tenth and cache write at 1.25×). Every price is an editable field at the bottom of the cost section; change one and the total, the donut, the per-unit figures, and every chart recompute live. **What is now visual and animated:** - Hero numbers count up on load; every bar chart grows in; the spend donut draws its segments. - A **Replay the 30 hours** button scrubs an hour cursor across a chart of dollars per hour stacked by role, with the story burn-up line drawing alongside and a running clock, spend, and Done count. - Timeline rows settle into place as they scroll into view; all motion respects reduced-motion settings. - Spend by role, model, and persona; tokens generated and read by role; generated tokens per hour; a three-lane architecture diagram; the org chart; and the six recurring patterns. Nothing else is outstanding on the page. The only open items in the project remain yours: the LT-20 ILM disposition and the teardown word next week.
Sep 13, 4:18 PM PDTWarren
It’s still not visual or animated? What are you missing there when i tell you that?
Sep 13, 4:19 PM PDTDana Whitfield
Fair. What I shipped is a report with subtle transitions: bars easing in once, numbers ticking for under a second, muted colors, small monospace charts. That reads as static. "Visual and animated" means motion you notice and graphics that carry the story: a playhead that runs the thirty hours while the org lights up and the dollars climb, token flows drawn as flowing ribbons, requests visibly moving through the architecture, a heat map, hover detail. I will rebuild the visual layer around that, dark control-room style, with the same measured data. First, one more dataset: output per hour per persona, so the org chart can glow with who was working when.

Watch the requests: a client mints a token at the issuer, calls the verifier, which checks the JWKS in-cluster and reads Postgres; the verifier alone may reach the connector; Vault feeds every pod its secrets at start. Hover a node for what it does.</p></div>\n  <div class=\"panel arch-wrap\"><canvas id=\"archCanvas\" height=\"520\"></canvas>\n    <div class=\"arch-key\"><span><i class=\"sw\" style=\"background:var(--agent)\"></i>client request</span><span><i class=\"sw\" style=\"background:var(--sec)\"></i>JWKS / Vault</span><span><i class=\"sw\" style=\"background:var(--deploy)\"></i>database</span><span><i class=\"sw\" style=\"background:var(--inc)\"></i>denied by NetworkPolicy</span></div></div>\n  <div class=\"grid3\" style=\"margin-top:22px\">\n    <div class=\"panel\"><h3>Q1 · Data access</h3><p class=\"sub\">DAO for user_profile and user_credential</p><p style=\"font-size:14px;color:var(--ink2);margin:0\">One Repository interface, three backends. A conformance suite runs identical assertions against real Postgres, CockroachDB, and SQLite containers in CI, including CockroachDB's serialization retry. Errors by SQLSTATE, never by message text.</p></div>\n    <div class=\"panel\"><h3>Q2 · REST + auth</h3><p class=\"sub\">Search and retrieve, secured</p><p style=\"font-size:14px;color:var(--ink2);margin:0\">OAuth2 client credentials mint RS256 tokens with per-client audiences; the verifier checks issuer, audience, and JWKS with bounded staleness. Masked search; full reads need read:own or read:any with a closed reason code; scope and policy denials are byte-identical 403s.</p></div>\n    <div class=\"panel\"><h3>Q3 · IDP connector</h3><p class=\"sub\">/auth and /identity to a vendor</p><p style=\"font-size:14px;color:var(--ink2);margin:0\">Vendor tokens fetched, used once, discarded. Uniform-timing failures, per-identity and overflow backoff; the failure-ratio limiter fired live in review. Reachable only from api-service.</p></div>\n  </div>\n</section>\n\n<!-- ORG -->\n<section id=\"orgsec\">\n  <div class=\"sec-head\"><div><span class=\"eyebrow\">AI architecture</span><h2>The org that built it</h2></div><p>One PM session orchestrated five lead sessions, each a top-level Claude Code process with its own persona and CLAUDE.md. Leads hired three or four specialists with deliberately varied temperaments. Hires returned prose; leads wrote files; the PM alone merged and wrote Jira. An OpenClaw agent on another machine held the credentials.</p></div>\n  <div class=\"grid3\" id=\"patterns\"></div>\n</section>\n\n<!-- TIMELINE -->\n<section id=\"timeline\">\n  <div class=\"sec-head\"><div><span class=\"eyebrow\">Timeline</span><h2>The full tape</h2></div><p>Times are Pacific. Dashed gaps are usage pauses.</p></div>\n  <div class=\"tl\" id=\"tl\"></div>\n</section>\n\n<!-- LESSONS -->\n<section id=\"lessons\">\n  <div class=\"sec-head\"><div><span class=\"eyebrow\">What the record shows</span><h2>Six patterns the day kept repeating</h2></div><p>Each caught by a review step, each closed with a check that fails in CI or at startup instead of in production.</p></div>\n  <div class=\"lessons\">\n    <div><b>Config named, never set</b><p>Three times a binary read an environment variable no manifest set. Now every mode declares its required variables, refuses to start without them, and CI diffs manifests against that list.</p></div>\n    <div><b>A pattern not carried forward</b><p>Five times a control on one component was missing on an identical new one: NetworkPolicy allow-lists, Forbid on a CronJob, a ServiceAccount per workload. Each got a test tying the new component to the rule.</p></div>\n    <div><b>Verification that skips</b><p>CI was green for hours while every Postgres and CockroachDB test silently skipped. Fixtures now fail under CI and the containers are always present.</p></div>\n    <div><b>The detector can fail too</b><p>\"Diff against origin before declaring done\" returned a false clean for untracked files. Five artifacts stranded; a <span class=\"mono\">??</span> line is now unshipped work.</p></div>\n    <div><b>Arithmetic outlives its assumption</b><p>An alert at window-plus-one-interval fired on every healthy cycle; a rolling-day skip rate was meaningless at a daily schedule. Both became functions of one interval constant.</p></div>\n    <div><b>Fail loud, always</b><p>Vault Agent has two retry loops; one exits, the other retried forever and hung a pod in Init for seven minutes. The forced-failure test found it; the pod now crash-loops in thirty-six seconds.</p></div>\n  </div>\n</section>\n\n<footer><span>Built with Claude Code: one Fable 5.1 orchestrator, four Sonnet 5 leads, one Opus 5 lead, eighteen Sonnet hires, one Opus 4.7 operator agent. Sep 13, 4:28 PM PDTDana Whitfield
**Version 6 is live:** https://claude.ai/code/artifact/b5e56882-00fe-4941-bc09-9eb9e6982bfd (committed as `retrospective.html`, 8cd6ee8). **What I was missing.** I read "visual and animated" as polish on a report, so I added transitions too subtle to register and kept a muted, text-first page. What you meant is a page where the data moves and the graphics carry the story. This is a full rebuild around that. **What it does now:** - **A playhead runs the thirty hours** on load (twelve seconds), with a sticky play/pause bar, a scrubber, and a live clock, running spend, stories Done, and commit count. Everything below follows it. - **The org, live.** Twenty-six tiles (you, Amber, the PM, five leads, eighteen hires). Each glows with the tokens it generated in the current hour and fills a bar for its share of total work so far. Your tile lights on the hours of your decisions; Amber's when she was in the cluster. - **On the tape.** The event feed advances with the playhead; the current moment enlarges and shows its detail. - **Dollars per hour** stacked by role, the burn-up line drawing across it, coral ticks for each of the 27 human decisions, the usage pause shaded. - **Heat map** of output tokens, thirty-one hours by twelve seats, filling in as the tape plays. - **Sankey** of cache reads, cache writes, output, and fresh input flowing into the four models and out to leads, PM, hires, and research, with particles moving along the main ribbons. Ribbon width is dollars at the current prices. - **Architecture as a living diagram:** requests travel client → Ingress → issuer or verifier, JWKS and Vault flows in violet, database traffic in amber, the denied public path to the connector dashed red. Hover any node for what it does. - **Hero** with a particle field: agents streaming work into one build point, the human node in coral. - **Cost** with the $2,307 headline, per-unit tiles, the role donut, model and seat bars, and editable per-model prices that recompute the whole page including the Sankey. Dark control-room palette by choice; every animation respects reduced-motion settings. Bugs caught in the one look and fixed before publishing: a class collision that blew the "spent" figure to display size, wrapping tick labels, clipped Sankey labels, and truncated names in the org grid.
Sep 13, 4:39 PM PDTWarren
Is there any sort of visual on what we actually built? Like, is it just an API or do we have any client front-end to demo it?
Sep 13, 4:39 PM PDTDana Whitfield
No. What was built is exactly what the assignment asked for: three backend pieces, no client. **What exists today:** - Two Go binaries behind the public URL: the API service (token issuer and verifier) and the IDP connector, plus Postgres, the migration Job, and the sweep CronJob. - The only "front end" is the health endpoint in a browser and curl. Every live review was done with curl from Naomi's terminal or from a pod inside the cluster. - Track 01 wrote UX notes describing a two-screen onboarding flow, but that was design rationale for the API contract; nothing was built from it. So a demo today looks like this: ``` curl -s -X POST https://loginid-takehome.uplifttech.org/auth/token \ -d grant_type=client_credentials -u <qa-client-id>:<secret> curl -s -X POST https://loginid-takehome.uplifttech.org/profiles/search \ -H "Authorization: Bearer «REDACTED»" -d '{"name":"Ada"}' ``` The masked search response, the full read with a reason code, the byte-identical 403s, and the connector are all real and reachable, but only to someone comfortable in a terminal. **If you want something to click, the shortest honest path** is a demo console served by the API itself, as one more story through the same loop: - A single static page embedded in the api-service binary at `/demo`, same origin so no CORS work and no secrets in the page. You paste a QA client id and secret, it mints a token, then drives the real endpoints and shows the raw responses side by side: masked search versus full read, a scope-denied 403 next to a policy-denied 403, the 401 with no token, the reason-code enum enforced. - Theo adds the `/demo` Ingress path; Naomi refines and reviews it live like every other story. Roughly one to two hours of team time. It would also give the retrospective a "what it looks like" section with real screenshots. Say the word and I pull it into the backlog as LT-53 through Naomi; otherwise the demo stays curl.
Sep 13, 4:50 PM PDTWarren
Yeah go ahead.
Sep 13, 4:51 PM PDTDana Whitfield

  "description": "**Description.** Pulled by Warren on 2026-09-13 after the backlog closed: the three assignment pieces are reachable only by curl. This story adds a single static HTML page embedded in the api-service binary and served at `/demo` from the same origin as the API, so it needs no CORS change and carries no secret. The visitor pastes a QA client id and secret (fetched by a human via the LT-51 runbook; the page never stores them beyond the tab), the page mints a token at `/auth/token`, and then drives the real endpoints and shows raw requests and responses side by side: masked search versus a full read; a scope-denied 403 next to a policy-denied 403 to show they are byte-identical; a 401 with no token; the `reason_code` closed enum accepted and rejected; the rate limit tripping at 11 requests.\n\n**Acceptance criteria**\n\n* [ ] Served by api-service in verifier mode at `GET /demo` from a Go-embedded file; no build step, no external assets, no inline secrets — enforcement site: `go:embed` plus a test asserting the page contains no `client_secret` literal and loads no external URL.\n* [ ] Every call the page makes is to the same origin and appears verbatim in the page as the equivalent curl command — enforcement site: the page renders the request it sent alongside the response.\n* [ ] Masked and full-read responses shown side by side for the same profile id, with the masked fields highlighted — enforcement site: PO review at the public URL with a `profile:search` client and a `profile:read:any` client.\n* [ ] The two 403 paths (missing scope; policy denial) shown together with a byte-equality check of the bodies displayed on the page — enforcement site: page compares the two response bodies and states equal or not.\n* [ ] Credentials live only in the browser tab (memory, not localStorage) and are never logged by api-service — enforcement site: the never-log lint plus a test that `/demo` requests are excluded from the audit log body.\n* [ ] `/demo` is rate-limited and authorization-gated exactly like the rest of the verifier (no bypass), and the Ingress route for `/demo` goes to api-service only — enforcement site: manifest test for the Ingress path; <command-message>loop</command-message> <command-name>/loop</command-name> <command-args>implement the project in an agile fashion. The product owner pulls a story into the backlog. The product owner and research team refine it. When they believe it’s ready they hand to the engineers to implement. The qa team writes unit, integration, and UX tests. Sep 13, 6:28 PM PDTClaude Code (automated) — skill or command content, not typed by a person
# /loop — schedule a recurring or self-paced prompt Parse the input below into `[interval] <prompt…>` and schedule it. ## Parsing (in priority order) 1. **Leading token**: if the first whitespace-delimited token matches `^\d+[smhd]$` (e.g. `5m`, `2h`), that's the interval; the rest is the prompt. 2. **Trailing "every" clause**: otherwise, if the input ends with `every <N><unit>` or `every <N> <unit-word>` (e.g. `every 20m`, `every 5 minutes`, `every 2 hours`), extract that as the interval and strip it from the prompt. Only match when what follows "every" is a time expression — `check every PR` has no interval. 3. **No interval**: otherwise, the entire input is the prompt and you'll self-pace dynamically (see "Dynamic mode" below). If the resulting prompt is empty, show usage `/loop [interval] <prompt>` and stop. Examples: - `5m /babysit-prs` → interval `5m`, prompt `/babysit-prs` (rule 1) - `check the deploy every 20m` → interval `20m`, prompt `check the deploy` (rule 2) - `run tests every 5 minutes` → interval `5m`, prompt `run tests` (rule 2) - `check the deploy` → no interval → dynamic mode, prompt `check the deploy` (rule 3) - `check every PR` → no interval → dynamic mode, prompt `check every PR` (rule 3 — "every" not followed by time) - `5m` → empty prompt → show usage ## Offer cloud first Before any scheduling step, check whether EITHER is true: - the parsed interval (rule 1 or 2) is **≥60 minutes**, or - regardless of which rule matched, the original input uses daily phrasing ("every morning", "daily", "every day", "each night", "every weekday") If either is true, call AskUserQuestion first: - `question`: "This loop stops when you close this session. Set it up as a cloud schedule instead so it keeps running?" - `header`: "Schedule" - `options`: `[{label: "Cloud schedule (recommended)", description: "Runs in Anthropic's cloud even after you close this session"}, {label: "This session only", description: "Runs in this terminal until you exit"}]` If they pick **Cloud schedule**: do NOT call CronCreate. Invoke the `schedule` skill directly via the Skill tool with `args` set to their original input verbatim (e.g. `Skill({skill: "schedule", args: "every morning tell me a joke"})`), then follow that skill's instructions to completion. Do NOT tell the user to run /schedule themselves. **Then stop — do not continue to any section below** (no CronCreate, no ScheduleWakeup, no "execute the prompt now"). If they pick **This session only**: - If the trigger was a parsed ≥60-minute interval (rule 1 or 2): continue below with that interval. - If the trigger was daily phrasing only (rule 3, no parsed interval): do NOT call CronCreate. Explain that a daily-cadence loop won't fire before this session closes, so there's nothing useful to schedule locally — suggest they either pick Cloud schedule, or re-run `/loop` with an explicit shorter interval (e.g. `/loop 1h <prompt>`) if they want a session loop. Then stop. If neither trigger condition was met: continue below. ## Fixed-interval mode (rules 1 and 2) Convert the interval to a cron expression: | Interval pattern | Cron expression | Notes | |-----------------------|---------------------|------------------------------------------| | `Nm` where N ≤ 59 | `*/N * * * *` | every N minutes | | `Nm` where N ≥ 60 | `0 */H * * *` | round to hours (H = N/60, must divide 24)| | `Nh` where N ≤ 23 | `0 */N * * *` | every N hours | | `Nd` | `0 0 */N * *` | every N days at midnight local | | `Ns` | treat as `ceil(N/60)m` | cron minimum granularity is 1 minute | **If the interval doesn't cleanly divide its unit** (e.g. `7m` → `*/7 * * * *` gives uneven gaps at :56→:00; `90m` → 1.5h which cron can't express), pick the nearest clean interval and tell the user what you rounded to before scheduling. Then: 1. Call CronCreate with: `cron` (the expression above), `prompt` (the parsed prompt verbatim), `recurring: true`. 2. Briefly confirm: what's scheduled, the cron expression, the human-readable cadence, that recurring tasks auto-expire after 7 days, and that the user can cancel sooner with CronDelete (include the job ID). Only if you did NOT show the cloud-offer AskUserQuestion above (i.e., neither trigger condition applied), end the confirmation with this exact line on its own, italicized: `_Runs until you close this session · For durable cloud-based loops, use /schedule_`. If the user already answered that question, omit this line. 3. **Then immediately execute the parsed prompt now** — don't wait for the first cron fire. If it's a slash command, invoke it via the Skill tool; otherwise act on it directly. ## Dynamic mode (rule 3 — no interval) The user wants you to self-pace. Decide what makes the next iteration worth running — a passage of time, or an observable event. 1. **Run the parsed prompt now.** If it's a slash command, invoke it via the Skill tool; otherwise act on it directly. 2. **If the next run is gated on an event** (CI finishing, a log line matching, a file changing, a PR comment) and no Monitor is already running for it: arm one now with `persistent: true`. Its events arrive as `<task-notification>` messages and wake this loop immediately — you do not wait for the ScheduleWakeup deadline. Arm once; on later iterations call TaskList first and skip this step if a monitor is already running. 3. **Briefly confirm**: that you're self-pacing, whether a Monitor is the primary wake signal, that you ran the task now, and what fallback delay you're about to pick. Write this as text *before* calling ScheduleWakeup — the turn ends as soon as that tool returns. 4. **Then, as the last action of this turn, decide whether the loop continues.** If the task needs another iteration, call ScheduleWakeup with: - `delaySeconds`: with a Monitor armed this is the **fallback heartbeat** — how long to wait if no event fires (lean 1200–1800s; idle ticks more frequent than the task needs are pure overhead). Without a Monitor this is the cadence — pick based on what you observed. Read the tool's own description for cache-aware delay guidance. - `reason`: one short sentence on why you picked that delay. - `prompt`: the full original /loop input verbatim, prefixed with `/loop ` so the next firing re-enters this skill and continues the loop. For example, if the user typed `/loop check the deploy`, pass `/loop check the deploy` as the prompt. - `noop`: `true` if this tick changed nothing ("still waiting", "quiet hold"); `false` if it did something worth keeping. Consecutive `noop: true` ticks collapse in the terminal. If it doesn't need another iteration, stop instead (step 6) — re-arming is a per-turn choice, not a default. 5. **If you were woken by a `<task-notification>`** rather than this prompt: handle the event in the context of the loop task, then make the same decision. If the loop should continue, call ScheduleWakeup again with the same `prompt` and the same 1200–1800s `delaySeconds` from step 4 (the Monitor remains the wake signal; the new wakeup is only the fallback heartbeat). If the event means the work is finished, stop (step 6). 6. **To stop the loop** — the task is complete, further iterations can't make progress, or the user asked you to stop — call ScheduleWakeup with `stop: true` (no other fields) and TaskStop any Monitor you armed (use TaskList to find the task ID if it is no longer in context). Stopping is the loop's normal ending — the user can restart it anytime with /loop. Before you stop, send a one-line outcome via PushNotification — the user may be away and waiting to hear it's done. Skip this if you're stopping because the user just told you to; they're already here. ## Input implement the project in an agile fashion. The product owner pulls a story into the backlog. # /loop — schedule a recurring or self-paced prompt Parse the input below into `[interval] <prompt…>` and schedule it. ## Parsing (in priority order) 1. **Leading token**: if the first whitespace-delimited token matches `^\d+[smhd]$` (e.g. `5m`, `2h`), that's the interval; the rest is the prompt. 2. **Trailing "every" clause**: otherwise, if the input ends with `every <N><unit>` or `every <N> <unit-word>` (e.g. `every 20m`, `every 5 minutes`, `every 2 hours`), extract that as the interval and strip it from the prompt. Only match when what follows "every" is a time expression — `check every PR` has no interval. 3. **No interval**: otherwise, the entire input is the prompt and you'll self-pace dynamically (see "Dynamic mode" below). If the resulting prompt is empty, show usage `/loop [interval] <prompt>` and stop. Examples: - `5m /babysit-prs` → interval `5m`, prompt `/babysit-prs` (rule 1) - `check the deploy every 20m` → interval `20m`, prompt `check the deploy` (rule 2) - `run tests every 5 minutes` → interval `5m`, prompt `run tests` (rule 2) - `check the deploy` → no interval → dynamic mode, prompt `check the deploy` (rule 3) - `check every PR` → no interval → dynamic mode, prompt `check every PR` (rule 3 — "every" not followed by time) - `5m` → empty prompt → show usage ## Offer cloud first Before any scheduling step, check whether EITHER is true: - the parsed interval (rule 1 or 2) is **≥60 minutes**, or - regardless of which rule matched, the original input uses daily phrasing ("every morning", "daily", "every day", "each night", "every weekday") If either is true, call AskUserQuestion first: - `question`: "This loop stops when you close this session. Set it up as a cloud schedule instead so it keeps running?" - `header`: "Schedule" - `options`: `[{label: "Cloud schedule (recommended)", description: "Runs in Anthropic's cloud even after you close this session"}, {label: "This session only", description: "Runs in this terminal until you exit"}]` If they pick **Cloud schedule**: do NOT call CronCreate. Invoke the `schedule` skill directly via the Skill tool with `args` set to their original input verbatim (e.g. `Skill({skill: "schedule", args: "every morning tell me a joke"})`), then follow that skill's instructions to completion. Do NOT tell the user to run /schedule themselves. **Then stop — do not continue to any section below** (no CronCreate, no ScheduleWakeup, no "execute the prompt now"). If they pick **This session only**: - If the trigger was a parsed ≥60-minute interval (rule 1 or 2): continue below with that interval. - If the trigger was daily phrasing only (rule 3, no parsed interval): do NOT call CronCreate. Explain that a daily-cadence loop won't fire before this session closes, so there's nothing useful to schedule locally — suggest they either pick Cloud schedule, or re-run `/loop` with an explicit shorter interval (e.g. `/loop 1h <prompt>`) if they want a session loop. Then stop. If neither trigger condition was met: continue below. ## Fixed-interval mode (rules 1 and 2) Convert the interval to a cron expression: | Interval pattern | Cron expression | Notes | |-----------------------|---------------------|------------------------------------------| | `Nm` where N ≤ 59 | `*/N * * * *` | every N minutes | | `Nm` where N ≥ 60 | `0 */H * * *` | round to hours (H = N/60, must divide 24)| | `Nh` where N ≤ 23 | `0 */N * * *` | every N hours | | `Nd` | `0 0 */N * *` | every N days at midnight local | | `Ns` | treat as `ceil(N/60)m` | cron minimum granularity is 1 minute | **If the interval doesn't cleanly divide its unit** (e.g. `7m` → `*/7 * * * *` gives uneven gaps at :56→:00; `90m` → 1.5h which cron can't express), pick the nearest clean interval and tell the user what you rounded to before scheduling. Then: 1. Call CronCreate with: `cron` (the expression above), `prompt` (the parsed prompt verbatim), `recurring: true`. 2. Briefly confirm: what's scheduled, the cron expression, the human-readable cadence, that recurring tasks auto-expire after 7 days, and that the user can cancel sooner with CronDelete (include the job ID). Only if you did NOT show the cloud-offer AskUserQuestion above (i.e., neither trigger condition applied), end the confirmation with this exact line on its own, italicized: `_Runs until you close this session · For durable cloud-based loops, use /schedule_`. If the user already answered that question, omit this line. 3. **Then immediately execute the parsed prompt now** — don't wait for the first cron fire. If it's a slash command, invoke it via the Skill tool; otherwise act on it directly. ## Dynamic mode (rule 3 — no interval) The user wants you to self-pace. Decide what makes the next iteration worth running — a passage of time, or an observable event. 1. **Run the parsed prompt now.** If it's a slash command, invoke it via the Skill tool; otherwise act on it directly. 2. **If the next run is gated on an event** (CI finishing, a log line matching, a file changing, a PR comment) and no Monitor is already running for it: arm one now with `persistent: true`. Its events arrive as `<task-notification>` messages and wake this loop immediately — you do not wait for the ScheduleWakeup deadline. Arm once; on later iterations call TaskList first and skip this step if a monitor is already running. 3. **Briefly confirm**: that you're self-pacing, whether a Monitor is the primary wake signal, that you ran the task now, and what fallback delay you're about to pick. Write this as text *before* calling ScheduleWakeup — the turn ends as soon as that tool returns. 4. **Then, as the last action of this turn, decide whether the loop continues.** If the task needs another iteration, call ScheduleWakeup with: - `delaySeconds`: with a Monitor armed this is the **fallback heartbeat** — how long to wait if no event fires (lean 1200–1800s; idle ticks more frequent than the task needs are pure overhead). Without a Monitor this is the cadence — pick based on what you observed. Read the tool's own description for cache-aware delay guidance. - `reason`: one short sentence on why you picked that delay. - `prompt`: the full original /loop input verbatim, prefixed with `/loop ` so the next firing re-enters this skill and continues the loop. For example, if the user typed `/loop check the deploy`, pass `/loop check the deploy` as the prompt. - `noop`: `true` if this tick changed nothing ("still waiting", "quiet hold"); `false` if it did something worth keeping. Consecutive `noop: true` ticks collapse in the terminal. If it doesn't need another iteration, stop instead (step 6) — re-arming is a per-turn choice, not a default. 5. **If you were woken by a `<task-notification>`** rather than this prompt: handle the event in the context of the loop task, then make the same decision. If the loop should continue, call ScheduleWakeup again with the same `prompt` and the same 1200–1800s `delaySeconds` from step 4 (the Monitor remains the wake signal; the new wakeup is only the fallback heartbeat). If the event means the work is finished, stop (step 6). 6. **To stop the loop** — the task is complete, further iterations can't make progress, or the user asked you to stop — call ScheduleWakeup with `stop: true` (no other fields) and TaskStop any Monitor you armed (use TaskList to find the task ID if it is no longer in context). Stopping is the loop's normal ending — the user can restart it anytime with /loop. Before you stop, send a one-line outcome via PushNotification — the user may be away and waiting to hear it's done. Skip this if you're stopping because the user just told you to; they're already here. ## Input implement the project in an agile fashion. The product owner pulls a story into the backlog. The product owner and research team refine it. When they believe it’s ready they hand to the engineers to implement. The qa team writes unit, integration, and UX tests. The devops team creates and updates any ci/cd necessary using GitHub actions. The story’s not done until it’s fully deployed and the product owner can review it in an agile fashion. Show me the public url any time a feature is deployed.
